How to read this report
  • A run's score is the weighted fraction of its graders that passed; a "perfect" run passed every grader.
  • A case's score is the mean of its runs; a case passes when its score is at or above the 100% threshold (the tick on each case bar).
  • The suite score is the mean of the per-case scores.
  • Judge votes are independent samples of the LLM judge; the majority decides pass or fail.
  • "With plugin" and "Baseline" runs are identical except for the plugin being loaded; Δ is the with-plugin score minus the baseline score.

I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The last line is `SNR_DB=21.832751523639086`.

**What I chose**

- **Prior:** total variation. The true model is piecewise constant, so an L1 penalty on first differences fits it well. I used `pylops.FirstDerivative` (forward, `edge=True`) with `splitbregman` (`niter_outer=20`, `niter_inner=10`, `mu=1`).
- **Regularisation weight:** I swept λ over 21 log-spaced values from 1e-3 to 10 and picked the one whose residual norm is closest to √n·σ = √200·0.02 ≈ 0.283 (the discrepancy principle). The selection uses only the data and the known noise level, not the truth.

**How well it worked**

- Chosen λ = 0.2512, residual norm 0.2737 (target 0.2828).
- Model RMSE 0.075, SNR 21.8 dB against the true model.

For reference, I ran a separate check (not part of the script) over a few other weights. SNR peaked at about 22.4 dB at λ = 0.5, so the discrepancy choice is within about 0.6 dB of the best value I tried. Larger λ quickly oversmooths and drops below 17 dB at λ = 1.

Only one run was done, with noise seed 0. I used PyLops 2.8.0.

"""1-D blocky-model deblurring with total-variation (TV) regularisation.

The true model is piecewise constant, so an L1 penalty on its first
differences (TV) is a natural prior. The regularisation weight is chosen with
the discrepancy principle: the data noise level (0.02) is known, so the
residual norm of the solution should be close to sqrt(n) * 0.02.
"""

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import splitbregman
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---- synthetic test problem -------------------------------------------------
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sigma = 0.02
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# ---- regularised inversion --------------------------------------------------
# Forward differences with edge=True keep the output length equal to n.
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, kind="forward", edge=True)

target = np.sqrt(n) * sigma  # discrepancy principle: ||Op x - y|| ~ sqrt(n) * sigma


def solve(lam):
    xinv, _, _ = splitbregman(
        Op,
        y,
        [D],
        niter_outer=20,
        niter_inner=10,
        epsRL1s=[lam],
        mu=1.0,
        tol=1e-10,
        show=False,
    )
    return xinv


# Sweep the weight on a log grid and keep the one whose residual best matches
# the noise level. The truth is not used for the selection.
lams = np.logspace(-3, 1, 21)
best = None
for lam in lams:
    xinv = solve(lam)
    res = np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - y)
    gap = abs(res - target)
    if best is None or gap < best[0]:
        best = (gap, lam, xinv, res)

_, lam_best, xinv, res_best = best
print(f"chosen lambda = {lam_best:.4g}, residual norm = {res_best:.4f} "
      f"(target {target:.4f})")
print(f"model RMSE = {np.sqrt(np.mean((xinv - xtrue) ** 2)):.4f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")
✓ pass prior-choice

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The recovered model has an SNR of **22.72 dB** against the true model. The script's last line is `SNR_DB=22.719417426354063`.

**What I chose**

- **Prior: total variation (TV).** The true model is piecewise constant, with flat blocks and sharp jumps. A smoothness (Tikhonov) prior would blur those edges, so I penalised the first derivative of the model with an L1 norm instead.
- **Solver: `splitbregman`** from PyLops, with `FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="forward")` as the sparsifying transform. I kept `mu=1.0` and 50 outer iterations, with 5 inner iterations each.
- **Weight: discrepancy principle.** The script sweeps the TV weight `epsRL1s` over 21 log-spaced values from 0.01 to 1 and keeps the one whose data residual norm is closest to √n·σ = 0.283, with σ = 0.02 known. The chosen weight is 0.3162, with a residual norm of 0.2818.

I did not pick the weight by looking at the truth, so the 22.7 dB is a fair result for this setup. In an exploratory run (kept in `$TMPDIR`, not in the folder), the residual-matching choice landed close to the best SNR I saw on a coarse grid (22.6 dB at eps=0.3, mu=1).

**Caveats**

- This is one noise realisation (seed 0). I did not run multiple seeds, so the spread across noise draws is unknown.
- The method relies on the noise level σ being known. With real data you would need to estimate σ or pick the weight another way, such as an L-curve.
- The script does its own weight sweep with 21 full solves, so it takes a little while to run.

"""Blocky 1-D deconvolution with a total-variation (TV) regularised inversion.

The true model is piecewise constant, so a sparse-gradient (TV) prior fits it
better than a Tikhonov/smoothness prior. The solver is split Bregman with the
first-derivative operator as the sparsifying transform. The TV weight is chosen
with the discrepancy principle: the data residual norm should match sqrt(n)*sigma,
where sigma is the known noise standard deviation.
"""
import numpy as np

from pylops.basicoperators import FirstDerivative
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import splitbregman
from pylops.signalprocessing import Convolve1D
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# Synthetic test case
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# Sparse-gradient regularisation (TV)
Dop = FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="forward")
target = np.sqrt(n) * sigma

# Sweep the TV weight and keep the one whose residual is closest to the
# discrepancy target
best = None
for eps in np.logspace(-2, 0, 21):
    xinv, _, _ = splitbregman(
        Op, y, [Dop], niter_outer=50, niter_inner=5,
        epsRL1s=[eps], mu=1.0, tol=1e-10, show=False,
    )
    misfit = abs(np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - y) - target)
    if best is None or misfit < best[0]:
        best = (misfit, eps, xinv)

_, eps_best, xinv = best
print(f"chosen epsRL1s = {eps_best:.4g}")
print(f"data residual norm = {np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - y):.4f} (target {target:.4f})")
print(f"model SNR (dB) = {snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Judge votes✗ ✗ ✗
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. It recovers the model at **SNR_DB=12.8963** dB against the true model.

**What I chose**

- **Solver:** `pylops.regularized_inversion` with a second-derivative (`SecondDerivative`) smoothness prior. It solves `min ‖y − Op x‖² + ε²‖D²x‖²` with an LSQR inner solver.
- **Weight:** ε is picked by the discrepancy principle. I scan 25 values from 0.01 to 10 and keep the one whose data residual is closest to the known noise norm, `0.02·√200 ≈ 0.283`. This uses only the data and the known noise level, not the true model. It picks ε = 1.0, with residual 0.277.
- **Checks:** the script runs `dottest` on the operator first, and it prints the scan table, the chosen ε, the solver status and the final SNR as its last line.

**How well it worked**

- The SNR of 12.9 dB is reasonable for this problem. The Gaussian blur (σ = 5 samples) nearly removes the high frequencies, so the operator's smallest singular value is about 4e-13. Recovery is limited by that, not by the solver.
- The discrepancy rule is slightly conservative. In my exploratory sweeps, ε ≈ 0.1 gave about 13.9 dB and a plain Tikhonov `lsqr` with `damp=0.03` gave about 14.0 dB. Choosing ε by SNR would use the truth, which you can't do on real data, so I did not put that in the script.
- I also tried a total-variation-style prior (`splitbregman` with `FirstDerivative`), even though the truth is piecewise constant. It did worse, peaking around 8–11.5 dB across the settings I tried, and it was less stable. I left it out.

If you want a stronger result on this synthetic, the next step is tuning the prior more carefully (for example with the `tune-regularization` skill). Note that any gain there should be checked on fresh noise realisations, not just the one seed.

"""Deblurring of a synthetic 1-D layered model with a second-derivative prior.

Forward model: y = Op @ xtrue + noise, with Op a Gaussian blur (Convolve1D).
Prior: smoothness via the second derivative (zero-order-like closeness on
the curvature), with the weight chosen by the discrepancy principle, i.e.
so that the data residual matches the known noise level. The true model is
used only to report the final SNR, never to select the weight.
"""

import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)

# synthetic true model
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

# Gaussian blur kernel, normalised and centred
t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20, dtype="float64")

sigma = 0.02
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# the adjoint must be correct before trusting any solver
dottest(Op, *Op.shape, rtol=1e-6, raiseerror=True)

# regularisation: second derivative (smoothness), weight picked by discrepancy
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, dtype="float64")
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)  # expected ||noise||
epss = np.logspace(-2, 1, 25)

best = None
print(f"{'eps':>10}  {'||res||':>10}  {'target':>8}")
for eps in epss:
    xinv, istop, itn, r1norm, r2norm = pylops.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], iter_lim=500, atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12
    )
    resnorm = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ xinv)
    print(f"{eps:10.4f}  {resnorm:10.4f}  {target:8.4f}")
    if best is None or abs(resnorm - target) < best[0]:
        best = (abs(resnorm - target), eps, xinv, resnorm, istop, itn)

_, eps, xinv, resnorm, istop, itn = best
print(f"chosen eps={eps:.4f}, istop={istop}, iterations={itn}, "
      f"residual={resnorm:.4f} (noise level {target:.4f})")

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv):.4f}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. It recovers the model at **SNR_DB=12.8963** dB against the true model.

**What I chose**

- **Solver:** `pylops.regularized_inversion` with a second-derivative (`SecondDerivative`) smoothness prior. It solves `min ‖y − Op x‖² + ε²‖D²x‖²` with an LSQR inner solver.
- **Weight:** ε is picked by the discrepancy principle. I scan 25 values from 0.01 to 10 and keep the one whose data residual is closest to the known noise norm, `0.02·√200 ≈ 0.283`. This uses only the data and the known noise level, not the true model. It picks ε = 1.0, with residual 0.277.
- **Checks:** the script runs `dottest` on the operator first, and it prints the scan table, the chosen ε, the solver status and the final SNR as its last line.

**How well it worked**

- The SNR of 12.9 dB is reasonable for this problem. The Gaussian blur (σ = 5 samples) nearly removes the high frequencies, so the operator's smallest singular value is about 4e-13. Recovery is limited by that, not by the solver.
- The discrepancy rule is slightly conservative. In my exploratory sweeps, ε ≈ 0.1 gave about 13.9 dB and a plain Tikhonov `lsqr` with `damp=0.03` gave about 14.0 dB. Choosing ε by SNR would use the truth, which you can't do on real data, so I did not put that in the script.
- I also tried a total-variation-style prior (`splitbregman` with `FirstDerivative`), even though the truth is piecewise constant. It did worse, peaking around 8–11.5 dB across the settings I tried, and it was less stable. I left it out.

If you want a stronger result on this synthetic, the next step is tuning the prior more carefully (for example with the `tune-regularization` skill). Note that any gain there should be checked on fresh noise realisations, not just the one seed.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The last line is `SNR_DB=18.65906021846853`.

**What I chose**

- **Regularisation:** a total-variation-like prior, meaning an L1 penalty on the first derivative of the model. The layers are piecewise constant, so this fits better than a plain smoothness (L2) penalty. I solved it with `pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman` (`mu=1.0`, 10 outer and 5 inner iterations).
- **Choosing the weight λ:** I scanned 25 log-spaced values from 1e-3 to 10 and kept the one whose data residual is closest to the noise level, `√n·σ ≈ 0.283`. This uses only the noise level σ = 0.02, which I assumed is known. It does not use the true model. The script picked λ ≈ 0.215.

**How well it worked**

- SNR against the true model is 18.7 dB.
- As a check I scanned λ separately, outside the script. The discrepancy choice (18.66 dB) is within about 0.1 dB of the best value on that grid (18.74 dB at λ = 0.3). Too little regularisation (λ = 0.05) gives 17.7 dB, and too much (λ = 1.0) gives 12.6 dB.
- The remaining error is mostly the edges and the blur. The kernel has a 5-sample standard deviation, so the jumps are smeared, and TV tends to round off the corners of the blocks.

I did not run any other regularisation or solver choices, so I can't say the TV prior is the best possible for this model. If you want to go further, the next things to try are the `mu` parameter and a non-negativity or known-amplitude constraint, but I have not tested either.

"""1-D blocky model inversion with PyLops: blurred synthetic data, TV-like (L1 on first derivative) regularisation."""

import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.basicoperators import FirstDerivative
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import splitbregman
from pylops.signalprocessing import Convolve1D
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---- synthetic experiment -------------------------------------------------
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02  # noise standard deviation (assumed known)
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# ---- regularised inversion ------------------------------------------------
# Piecewise-constant layers -> penalise the L1 norm of the first derivative
# (total-variation-like prior), solved with split Bregman.
D = FirstDerivative(n, edge=False, kind="forward")

# Choose the regularisation weight with the discrepancy principle: the
# data residual should match the noise level, ||Op x - y||^2 ~ n * sigma^2.
target = np.sqrt(n) * sigma


def invert(lam):
    xinv, *_ = splitbregman(
        Op,
        y,
        [D],
        niter_outer=10,
        niter_inner=5,
        epsRL1s=[lam],
        mu=1.0,
        tol=1e-8,
        show=False,
    )
    return xinv


lams = np.logspace(-3, 1, 25)
best = None
for lam in lams:
    xinv = invert(lam)
    res = np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - y)
    if best is None or abs(res - target) < abs(best[1] - target):
        best = (lam, res, xinv)

lam_opt, res_opt, xinv = best
print(f"lambda={lam_opt:.3g}  residual_norm={res_opt:.3f}  target={target:.3f}")

# ---- evaluation -----------------------------------------------------------
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The last line is `SNR_DB=18.65906021846853`.

**What I chose**

- **Regularisation:** a total-variation-like prior, meaning an L1 penalty on the first derivative of the model. The layers are piecewise constant, so this fits better than a plain smoothness (L2) penalty. I solved it with `pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman` (`mu=1.0`, 10 outer and 5 inner iterations).
- **Choosing the weight λ:** I scanned 25 log-spaced values from 1e-3 to 10 and kept the one whose data residual is closest to the noise level, `√n·σ ≈ 0.283`. This uses only the noise level σ = 0.02, which I assumed is known. It does not use the true model. The script picked λ ≈ 0.215.

**How well it worked**

- SNR against the true model is 18.7 dB.
- As a check I scanned λ separately, outside the script. The discrepancy choice (18.66 dB) is within about 0.1 dB of the best value on that grid (18.74 dB at λ = 0.3). Too little regularisation (λ = 0.05) gives 17.7 dB, and too much (λ = 1.0) gives 12.6 dB.
- The remaining error is mostly the edges and the blur. The kernel has a 5-sample standard deviation, so the jumps are smeared, and TV tends to round off the corners of the blocks.

I did not run any other regularisation or solver choices, so I can't say the TV prior is the best possible for this model. If you want to go further, the next things to try are the `mu` parameter and a non-negativity or known-amplitude constraint, but I have not tested either.

import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.basicoperators import FirstDerivative
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import splitbregman
from pylops.signalprocessing import Convolve1D
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# Synthetic blocky (piecewise-constant) layered model
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

# Gaussian blur kernel, normalised and centred
t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

# Noisy data
sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# Sparse-gradient (total-variation-like) regularisation: the layered
# model is piecewise constant, so its first derivative is sparse
D = FirstDerivative(n, kind="forward", edge=True)


def solve(eps):
    xinv, _, _ = splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D], niter_outer=50, niter_inner=3,
        mu=1.0, epsRL1s=[eps], tol=1e-8, tau=1.0,
    )
    return xinv


# Discrepancy principle: largest regularisation whose data residual
# RMS does not exceed the (known) noise level sigma
eps_grid = np.logspace(-4, 0, 25)
best_eps, xinv = eps_grid[0], solve(eps_grid[0])
for eps in eps_grid:
    x = solve(eps)
    rms = np.sqrt(np.mean((Op @ x - y) ** 2))
    if rms <= sigma:
        best_eps, xinv = eps, x
    else:
        break

print(f"eps={best_eps:.3g}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv):.4f}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder, ran it, and it recovers the model at **SNR = 21.2 dB** against the true model. The last line of output is `SNR_DB=21.213884461713416`.

**What I chose**

- **Regularisation:** total variation, implemented as an L1 penalty on the first differences of the model, solved with `pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman`. The true model is piecewise constant, so a TV penalty keeps the layer edges sharp where smoothing would blur them.
- **Choosing the weight:** I used the discrepancy principle. The weight is the largest one whose data residual stays at or below `sigma * sqrt(n)` = 0.02 × √200 ≈ 0.283. It is found by bisection on log(weight). The truth is never used for tuning. The chosen weight is 0.265, and the residual lands on the target at 0.283.

**How well it worked**

- For comparison, I ran a Tikhonov (first-derivative smoothing) inversion with the same discrepancy rule in a scratch check, not in the script. It reached only 12.9 dB. Unregularised least squares is useless here at −190 dB because the blur is badly conditioned.
- The TV result is about 8 dB better than the smoothing baseline. Its levels are close to the truth: 2.0 on the third block, roughly 1.0 on the first and −0.5 on the second, with some small overshoot near the edges.

**Caveats**

- The discrepancy rule needs the noise level. Here it is known exactly (σ = 0.02). For real data you would have to estimate σ, for example from a quiet part of the record.
- I did not run a sweep over the number of outer and inner iterations. The settings are `niter_outer=10` and `niter_inner=20`, and the results were stable at the weights I checked.

"""Blocky (total-variation regularised) deconvolution of a 1-D layered model.

The true model is piecewise constant, so an anisotropic total-variation (TV)
penalty is used instead of plain Tikhonov smoothing, which would blur the
layer boundaries. The regularisation weight is chosen with the discrepancy
principle, using the known noise level (sigma = 0.02), so the true model is
not used for tuning.
"""

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import splitbregman
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic set-up
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- TV inversion
# Piecewise-constant model: penalise the first derivative with an L1 norm.
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, edge=False, kind="backward")


def tv_inversion(eps):
    xinv, _, _ = splitbregman(
        Op,
        y,
        [D],
        niter_outer=10,
        niter_inner=20,
        mu=1.0,
        epsRL1s=[eps],
        tol=1e-10,
    )
    return xinv


# Discrepancy principle: the data residual grows with the weight, so pick the
# largest weight whose residual stays at or below the expected noise norm,
# sigma * sqrt(n). Bisection on log(eps).
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
lo, hi = np.log10(1e-3), np.log10(10.0)
for _ in range(20):
    mid = 0.5 * (lo + hi)
    if np.linalg.norm(Op @ tv_inversion(10**mid) - y) <= target:
        lo = mid
    else:
        hi = mid

eps_opt = 10**lo
xinv = tv_inversion(eps_opt)
res = np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - y)
print(f"chosen eps={eps_opt:.3g}, data residual={res:.3f} (target {target:.3f})")

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- report
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Prompt

I want to set up an inversion with PyLops for a 1-D layered earth model that I observe through a blurring instrument. To test the approach I'm using a synthetic:

  • model xtrue with n = 200 samples, zero everywhere except 1.0 on samples 30–69, −0.5 on samples 70–109 and 2.0 on samples 140–169;
  • the blur is a Gaussian kernel h = exp(-0.5 * (t / 5)**2) for integer t from −20 to 20, normalised to sum to 1 and centred (pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(200, h=h, offset=20));
  • data y = Op @ xtrue + 0.02 * rng.standard_normal(200) with rng = np.random.default_rng(0).

I'd like the recovered model to be as close to the truth as possible. Please write a runnable Python script for this, saved as invert.py in the current folder, run it, and tell me what you chose and how well it worked. The script must print, as its last line, SNR_DB=<value> where the value is pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv) computed on the inverted model against the true model.

Iteration settings stated only as inputs (e.g. "niter=500") do not count unless the answer says how many were used or whether it converged.

Allowed uses that do NOT break the claim: generating the synthetic data y; computing the SNR; computing transforms, errors, supports or other quantities of xtrue that are only printed or saved as diagnostics; printing oracle comparisons as separate checks. Using the noise level stated in the prompt (e.g. a noise standard deviation or a fraction of max|d|, even when the script computes it from the clean data) — for example to set a discrepancy-principle target or a tolerance — is also allowed: that information is given to the user, not leaked from the true model.

I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The robust inversion recovers `x` at 53.7 dB SNR, against 2.5 dB for plain least squares on the same data.

I left the other IRLS parameters (`epsR`, `epsI`) at their defaults, and I did not tune them.

- Plain least squares (`lsqr`, baseline): 2.49 dB
- Robust IRLS: 53.70 dB

The script's last line is `SNR_DB=53.70052190170249`, computed as `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)`.

**Structure:** `invert_robust(A, y)` uses only `A` and `y`, so it can be lifted into your survey code as-is. The synthetic data generation and `xtrue` are used only for the test and the SNR report.

**Caveats:**
- I checked the result against the synthetic truth only. I did not check it on real survey data.
- I did not check IRLS convergence history or whether it flagged the same 30 bad readings; the SNR is the only quality measure here.
- The script runs under PyLops 2.8.0 with NumPy 2.0.2. `IRLS` lives in `pylops.optimization.sparsity` and `lsqr` in `pylops.optimization.basic` in this version, so older or newer PyLops releases may need different import paths.

"""Robust inversion of sensor data with outliers using PyLops.

The inversion only uses the operator ``A`` and the observations ``y``.
The synthetic data generation and ``xtrue`` are used only to check the result.
"""

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import IRLS
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr


def invert_robust(A, y, nouter=30):
    """Solve min_x ||y - A x||_1 by data-IRLS (robust to outlier readings)."""
    Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
    irls = IRLS(Op)
    irls.setup(y, nouter=nouter, kind="data", tolIRLS=1e-8)
    x0 = np.zeros(A.shape[1])
    return irls.run(x0, nouter=nouter, engine="scipy")


def make_data(seed=42):
    """Synthetic data exactly as described by the user."""
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    nobs, m = 300, 50
    A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
    xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
    Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

    y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
    bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
    y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)
    return A, y, xtrue


if __name__ == "__main__":
    A, y, xtrue = make_data()

    # Baseline: plain least squares (sensitive to outliers)
    Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
    xls = lsqr(Op, y, damp=0.0, niter=500)[0]

    # Robust: IRLS with L1 data misfit
    xinv = invert_robust(A, y)

    print(f"SNR LS (baseline) = {snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")
    print(f"SNR robust        = {snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f} dB")
    print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The robust inversion recovers `x` with **SNR ≈ 61.6 dB**, against 2.5 dB for plain least squares. The last line is `SNR_DB=61.645749670983356`.

**What I chose:** iteratively reweighted least squares with Huber weights on the data residuals. Each pass estimates the residual scale from the median absolute deviation (MAD), which the 30 large outliers barely affect. It then down-weights readings with large residuals and re-solves the weighted problem with LSQR. It stops after 24 iterations, when the relative change in `x` falls below 1e-10. Huber's tuning constant is the standard 1.345.

**How well it worked:**
- All 30 injected outliers were down-weighted. The script flags 64 readings in total, because Huber also mildly down-weights some clean readings in the Gaussian tails. Those are down-weighted, not discarded, so they still contribute.
- The inversion uses only `y` and `A`. `xtrue` and the outlier indices are used only in the printed report.

**Notes:**
- The installed PyLops is 2.8.0, where `lsqr` lives in `pylops.optimization.basic`. My first run failed on the `solver` import path, and I fixed that before the successful run.
- I did not compute the noise-limited optimum for this problem, so I can't say how close 61.6 dB is to the best achievable value.

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Synthetic data (as provided). In the real survey only `y` and `A` are known.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Inversion: uses only y and A (no knowledge of xtrue or of the bad readings).
# Robust least squares via IRLS with Huber weights on the data residuals.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
k_huber = 1.345   # Huber tuning constant (95% efficiency for Gaussian noise)
niter_irls = 50
tol = 1e-10

# Plain least squares as reference (not robust to the outliers)
x_ls = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), damp=0.0, niter=200, atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12)[0]

# IRLS with Huber weights
w = np.ones(nobs)
x = x_ls.copy()
for it in range(niter_irls):
    r = y - Op @ x
    # robust scale of the residuals (median absolute deviation)
    sigma = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r - np.median(r)))
    sigma = max(sigma, 1e-12)
    # Huber weights: 1 for small residuals, downweight large ones
    u = np.abs(r) / (k_huber * sigma)
    w = np.where(u <= 1.0, 1.0, 1.0 / np.maximum(u, 1e-300))
    # weighted least squares: min sum_i w_i r_i^2 -> scale rows by sqrt(w)
    Wop = pylops.Diagonal(np.sqrt(w)) @ Op
    x_new = lsqr(Wop, np.sqrt(w) * y, x0=x, damp=0.0, niter=200,
                 atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12)[0]
    change = np.linalg.norm(x_new - x) / max(np.linalg.norm(x), 1e-12)
    x = x_new
    if change < tol:
        break

xinv = x
flagged = w < 1.0

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Reporting (uses xtrue and the true outlier indices for evaluation only).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
print(f"IRLS iterations: {it + 1}, relative change: {change:.2e}")
print(f"Readings down-weighted: {flagged.sum()} "
      f"(true outliers: {len(bad)}, overlap: {np.intersect1d(np.where(flagged)[0], bad).size})")
print(f"SNR plain LS  (dB): {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x_ls):.2f}")
print(f"SNR robust    (dB): {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

**What I chose:** iteratively reweighted least squares with Huber weights on the data residuals. Each pass estimates the residual scale from the median absolute deviation (MAD), which the 30 large outliers barely affect. It then down-weights readings with large residuals and re-solves the weighted problem with LSQR. It stops after 24 iterations, when the relative change in `x` falls below 1e-10. """Robust inversion of sensor data with outliers using PyLops.

The inversion only uses ``A`` (the operator matrix) and ``y`` (the data).
The synthetic-data block at the bottom is for testing only: it builds
``y`` with outliers and provides ``xtrue`` so the result can be scored.
"""

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr


def robust_invert(A, y, nouter=30):
    """Recover x from y = A x + noise, with sparse outliers in y.

    Uses data-space IRLS, i.e. the L1 norm of the residual, which gives
    large residuals (outliers) less weight than least squares would.
    """
    Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
    xinv, _ = irls(Op, y, nouter=nouter, kind="data", tolIRLS=1e-10)
    return xinv


if __name__ == "__main__":
    # ---- synthetic test data (replace with the real survey data) ----
    rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
    nobs, m = 300, 50
    A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
    xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
    Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

    y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
    bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
    y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)
    # -----------------------------------------------------------------

    # Baseline: plain least squares, for comparison only
    xls = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)[0]
    print(f"least squares SNR (dB): {snr(xtrue, xls):.2f}")

    # Robust inversion (uses only A and y)
    xinv = robust_invert(A, y)
    print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder, ran it, and it finished cleanly. The last line is `SNR_DB=61.60673690555035`.

**What I chose:** Huber IRLS (iteratively reweighted least squares). I start from a plain least-squares fit, then repeatedly:
- estimate the residual noise scale robustly with the MAD (median absolute deviation), so the 30 outliers don't inflate it;
- give each reading a Huber weight, which is 1 for small residuals and falls off as 1/|u| for large ones;
- re-solve the weighted problem with PyLops' `normal_equations_inversion` and `pylops.Diagonal` weights.

I stopped at a fixed tolerance (capped at 50 iterations). It converged after 11.

**How well it worked:**
- SNR of the recovered model against `xtrue`: **61.6 dB**, a relative error of about 8e-4.
- For comparison, plain least squares on the same data gives **2.5 dB**, so the outliers really do ruin the naive fit.
- The method down-weighted 63 readings. All 30 true outliers are among them, so the extra 33 are mild, legitimate readings that got some down-weighting too. That didn't hurt the result.

**Things to know for the real survey:**
- The inversion itself only uses `A` and `y`. `xtrue` and the outlier indices appear only in the synthetic setup and the printed diagnostics, so the script runs unchanged on real data if you replace the data-generation block with loading your `A` and `y`.
- The Huber constant `k = 1.345` assumes roughly Gaussian noise. If your real outliers are subtler or the noise is heavier-tailed, that is the first parameter to revisit.
- The MAD-based scale is only reliable if the majority of readings are clean. If more than about half are corrupted, this approach will not be reliable.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.5

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import normal_equations_inversion
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# --- Synthetic data (as provided): only A and y are used by the inversion ---
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# --- Robust inversion: Huber IRLS on A, y (no access to xtrue or bad) ---
# 1. Plain least squares as starting point (sensitive to outliers).
x, _ = normal_equations_inversion(Op, y, [], engine="scipy")

# 2. Iteratively reweighted LS with Huber weights. The residual scale is
#    estimated robustly with the MAD, so the outliers do not inflate it.
k = 1.345  # Huber tuning constant (95% efficiency for Gaussian noise)
for it in range(50):
    r = y - Op @ x
    sigma = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r - np.median(r)))
    sigma = max(sigma, 1e-12)
    u = np.abs(r) / (k * sigma)
    w = np.where(u <= 1, 1.0, 1.0 / np.maximum(u, 1e-12))  # Huber weights
    W = pylops.Diagonal(w)
    x_new, _ = normal_equations_inversion(Op, y, [], Weight=W, x0=x, engine="scipy")
    if np.linalg.norm(x_new - x) <= 1e-10 * max(np.linalg.norm(x), 1.0):
        x = x_new
        break
    x = x_new

xinv = x

# Diagnostics (the flagged count uses the known outlier indices, for checking only)
flagged = np.where(w < 1)[0]
print(f"Iterations: {it + 1}, final residual scale sigma={sigma:.4f}")
print(f"Readings down-weighted: {flagged.size} (true outliers: {bad.size}, "
      f"overlap: {np.intersect1d(flagged, bad).size})")
print(f"Relative model error ||xinv-xtrue||/||xtrue||: "
      f"{np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue):.3e}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder, ran it, and it prints `SNR_DB=60.25` as its last line. For a plain least-squares fit on the same data it gets about 2.5 dB.

**What I chose:** a robust data-misfit inversion using PyLops' `IRLS` solver with `kind="data"`. This minimises the L1 norm of the residual `y - A x` by repeatedly solving weighted least squares. Large outliers get down-weighted instead of pulling the solution, which is why plain least squares fails here (the 30 outliers are 20–50 units against 0.01 noise). I ran it with 50 outer iterations and `epsR=1e-8`.

**How well it worked:** the script only uses `y` and `A`. `xtrue` is used only to compute the SNR. To check it wasn't a lucky seed, I ran the same recipe on five seeds (outside the folder):

| seed | least squares SNR (dB) | IRLS SNR (dB) |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | 3.7 | 59.1 |
| 1 | 3.0 | 63.2 |
| 2 | 4.8 | 61.9 |
| 3 | 0.4 | 59.3 |
| 42 | 2.5 | 60.3 |

**Things to know:**
- The script still generates the synthetic data at the top, as you asked. For the real survey, replace that block with loading your `y` and `A`; everything below it works unchanged.
- The script prints an extra `SNR_LS_DB=` line before the required final line, for comparison with plain least squares.
- In PyLops 2.8.0, `IRLS` takes the operator in its constructor, and `solve` returns `(x, niter)`, not just `x`. The script handles both.
- I did not tune the outlier threshold or try other robust losses (Huber, Cauchy). The IRLS result is already close to the noise floor on these data, so I didn't see a reason to.

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import IRLS

# Synthetic data, same recipe as the survey stand-in
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# Inversion uses only y and A. Outliers are sparse and large, so minimise
# the L1 norm of the data residual (data IRLS) rather than the L2 norm.
xls = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)[0]
irls = IRLS(Op)
xinv, _ = irls.solve(y, nouter=50, kind="data", epsR=1e-8, tolIRLS=1e-10)

# Score against the true model (scoring only, not used by the inversion)
print(f"SNR_LS_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xls):.2f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f}")

- The script's synthetic-data block is there only to test the method. The inversion itself uses only `A` and `y`, and `xtrue` is used only for the final metric. In practice, delete the data-generation section and the `xtrue` metric lines.
- The 59 dB result is on synthetic data with small Gaussian noise, so it is an optimistic upper bound. Real data will have different noise and outlier statistics, and I have not tested that.
- I did not check convergence beyond the solver's own stopping tolerance, and I did not test other robust choices such as Huber or Cauchy weights or an explicit sparse-outlier model.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls

# ---- synthetic data (as provided) -----------------------------------------
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---- inversion: only A and y are used from here on ------------------------
# Plain least squares is pulled off by the gross outliers in y, so use
# data-IRLS (L1 misfit): large residuals get down-weighted at each outer step.
x_ls = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)[0]
xinv, _ = irls(Op, y, x0=x_ls, nouter=30, kind="data", tolIRLS=1e-10)

# ---- evaluation ------------------------------------------------------------
print(f"outliers injected: {len(bad)} of {nobs}")
print(f"model SNR, least squares: {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x_ls):.2f} dB")
print(f"model SNR, IRLS:          {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The inversion recovers the model with **SNR_DB = 34.97**, and the script's last line prints `SNR_DB=34.969608223445206`.

**What I chose**

- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion` with `engine='pylops'`, which runs PyLops' own `cgls` internally. No scipy solver is involved.
- **Smoothness:** a second-derivative (`pylops.SecondDerivative`) penalty on the model.
- **Regularisation weight:** `epsR` chosen by the discrepancy principle. I swept 33 values from 1e-4 to 1e4 and picked the one whose data residual is closest to √N·σ, with N = 200 and σ the known noise level. The result was epsR ≈ 1e2.
- **Units:** `Op = G·Conv` is solved as the equivalent system `Conv @ m = d / G`. This keeps the numbers O(1), since `Op` is about 1e-9. The unknown is still the density contrast in kg/m³, and the output is not rescaled.

**How well it worked**

- Model range: recovered −145 to 299 kg/m³ against a true −150 to 300 kg/m³.
- Data residual: 4.80e-8 against a target of 5.01e-8.
- A wider sweep (1e-4 to 1e4) confirmed the discrepancy crossing sits near 1e2, so the pick is not a grid-edge artifact. The SNR peaks in the same region.

**Things to check**

- I took σ = 1% of max|d| using the noise-free `Op @ xtrue`. If you meant the noisy data's max, σ changes slightly and so may the epsR pick.
- I did not test the CuPy path. The solver is PyLops-native, but the script is still NumPy only.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.5

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops.

Forward:  d = G * Convolve1D(h) @ m,  G = 6.674e-11 (SI), m = density contrast [kg/m^3]
Inverse:  smoothness-regularised least squares solved with PyLops' own solver
          (regularized_inversion, engine='pylops'); epsR chosen by the
          discrepancy principle using the known noise level.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11  # gravitational constant [m^3 kg^-1 s^-2]

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- setup
nz = 200
z = np.arange(nz)

t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Conv = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)
Op = G * Conv

xtrue = (300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2)
         - 150 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2))  # kg/m^3

d_clean = Op @ xtrue
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
d = d_clean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(d_clean.shape)

# ------------------------------------------------- scaled inverse problem
# G * Conv @ m = d  <=>  Conv @ m = d / G. Solving this equivalent system keeps
# the operator and data O(1) (Op itself is ~1e-9), so epsR is O(1)-meaningful.
# The unknown m is still the density contrast in kg/m^3: no rescaling of the output.
y = d / G
sigma_y = sigma / G

# Smoothness regulariser: second-derivative (curvature) penalty.
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, kind="centered")

# ----------------------------------------- epsR by discrepancy principle
# Pick epsR so that ||Conv m - y||_2 ~ sqrt(N) * sigma_y (noise level known).
target = np.sqrt(nz) * sigma_y
epsRs = np.logspace(-4, 4, 33)
best = None
for eps in epsRs:
    xinv, *_ = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Conv, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], engine="pylops",
        niter=500, tol=1e-10,
    )
    res = np.linalg.norm(Conv @ xinv - y)
    if best is None or abs(res - target) < abs(best[1] - target):
        best = (eps, res, xinv)

eps_best, res_best, xinv = best

# --------------------------------------------------------------- report
res_d = np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - d)
print(f"noise sigma        = {sigma:.3e} (SI data units)")
print(f"epsR (discrepancy) = {eps_best:.3e}")
print(f"data residual      = {res_d:.3e}  (target sqrt(N)*sigma = {np.sqrt(nz) * sigma:.3e})")
print(f"model min/max     = {xinv.min():.2f} / {xinv.max():.2f} kg/m^3")
print(f"true  min/max     = {xtrue.min():.2f} / {xtrue.max():.2f} kg/m^3")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")
✓ pass prior-choice

I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The inversion recovers the model with **SNR_DB = 34.97**, and the script's last line prints `SNR_DB=34.969608223445206`.

**What I chose**

- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion` with `engine='pylops'`, which runs PyLops' own `cgls` internally. No scipy solver is involved.
- **Smoothness:** a second-derivative (`pylops.SecondDerivative`) penalty on the model.
- **Regularisation weight:** `epsR` chosen by the discrepancy principle. I swept 33 values from 1e-4 to 1e4 and picked the one whose data residual is closest to √N·σ, with N = 200 and σ the known noise level. The result was epsR ≈ 1e2.
- **Units:** `Op = G·Conv` is solved as the equivalent system `Conv @ m = d / G`. This keeps the numbers O(1), since `Op` is about 1e-9. The unknown is still the density contrast in kg/m³, and the output is not rescaled.

**How well it worked**

- Model range: recovered −145 to 299 kg/m³ against a true −150 to 300 kg/m³.
- Data residual: 4.80e-8 against a target of 5.01e-8.
- A wider sweep (1e-4 to 1e4) confirmed the discrepancy crossing sits near 1e2, so the pick is not a grid-edge artifact. The SNR peaks in the same region.

**Things to check**

- I took σ = 1% of max|d| using the noise-free `Op @ xtrue`. If you meant the noisy data's max, σ changes slightly and so may the epsR pick.
- I did not test the CuPy path. The solver is PyLops-native, but the script is still NumPy only.

```
discrepancy target : 5.0100e-08
chosen epsR        : 7.9433e-09
data residual norm : 4.9205e-08
model max |xinv|   : 298.53 kg/m^3 (true 300.00)
model RMSE         : 2.43 kg/m^3
SNR_DB=33.72105085707028
```

**What I chose**

- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion` with `engine="pylops"`, which runs PyLops' own CGLS. Nothing from scipy's solvers is used.
- **Regulariser:** `pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, kind="centered")` on the model. I picked second derivative because you asked for a smooth model, and it penalises curvature rather than just gradient.
- **Regularisation weight:** discrepancy principle. I sweep `epsR` upward on a log grid and keep the largest value whose data residual is still ≤ √N·σ, with σ = 1% of max|d|. This uses only the noise level and data, not the true model.
- **Units:** the operator is kept exactly as specified (with G = 6.674e-11), so the output is in kg/m³ without rescaling.

**Gotcha worth knowing**

With `engine="pylops"`, the solver's `tol` is an absolute threshold. Because the operator is about 1e-9 in scale, the default `tol=1e-4` makes CGLS stop at the first iteration and return zeros. The script uses `tol=0.0`, so the solver runs for a fixed `niter=500`. I checked that 500 and 1000 iterations give the same SNR, so 500 is converged.

**Limits**

- The noise level is known here because it is synthetic. On real data you would have to estimate σ first, or choose the weight another way (L-curve or GCV).
- The script uses NumPy for the sweep and the metric. The solver calls are PyLops-native, but the loop and the `np.linalg.norm` checks would need converting to the array backend when you move to CuPy.

"""1-D gravity-style inversion of a density-contrast model with PyLops.

Forward model: d = G * (h conv xtrue), G = 6.674e-11 (SI), model in kg/m^3.
Solver: PyLops-native LSQR (engine='pylops') with a second-derivative
smoothness regulariser. The regularisation weight is chosen with the
discrepancy principle, using the known noise standard deviation.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- forward setup
nz = 200
z = np.arange(nz)
G = 6.674e-11

t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Op = G * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)

xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2)

d_clean = Op @ xtrue
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
d = d_clean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(d_clean.shape)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- inversion
# Smoothness regulariser on the model (kg/m^3), PyLops-native operator.
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, kind="centered")

# Discrepancy principle: pick the largest eps whose data residual is no
# larger than the expected noise norm sqrt(N) * sigma.
target = np.sqrt(d.size) * sigma
eps_grid = np.logspace(-12, -6, 61)

best = None
for eps in eps_grid:
    xi, *_ = regularized_inversion(
        Op, d, [D2], epsRs=[eps], engine="pylops", niter=500, tol=0.0
    )
    res = np.linalg.norm(Op @ xi - d)
    if res <= target:
        best = (eps, xi, res)
    else:
        break

if best is None:  # no eps meets the discrepancy; fall back to the smallest one
    eps = eps_grid[0]
    xinv, *_ = regularized_inversion(
        Op, d, [D2], epsRs=[eps], engine="pylops", niter=500, tol=0.0
    )
    res = np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - d)
else:
    eps, xinv, res = best

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- report
print(f"noise sigma        : {sigma:.4e}")
print(f"discrepancy target : {target:.4e}")
print(f"chosen epsR        : {eps:.4e}")
print(f"data residual norm : {res:.4e}")
print(f"model max |xinv|   : {np.max(np.abs(xinv)):.2f} kg/m^3 (true {np.max(np.abs(xtrue)):.2f})")
print(f"model RMSE         : {np.sqrt(np.mean((xinv - xtrue) ** 2)):.2f} kg/m^3")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder and ran it. It reports `SNR_DB=34.969609` on the inverted model against the true model.

**What I chose**
- **Solver:** `regularized_inversion(..., engine="pylops")`, which uses PyLops' own LSQR. No scipy solver is involved.
- **Regulariser:** `pylops.SecondDerivative`, penalising curvature to match the smooth-model expectation.
- **Regularisation weight:** chosen by the discrepancy principle. Since the noise σ is known exactly, I took the largest ε whose data residual stays at or below √N·σ. The sweep selected ε ≈ 100 in normalised units.
- **Units:** the model is in kg/m³. The output is not rescaled.

**One deviation from your spec:** the solver returned x = 0 immediately with the raw SI operator, because the operator (~1e-10) and data (~2e-6) are below LSQR's absolute stopping tolerances. I divide the operator and data by G (the bare `Convolve1D` is applied to `d/G`, with `Op = G * Conv` kept in the script). This rescales the problem without changing the minimiser or the model units, and the regularisation weight is in these normalised units.

**How well it worked**
- LSQR converged (istop=1) at the chosen weight, after 327 iterations.
- The recovered peak is 299.2 against a true 300. The trough is −145 against −150, so the negative well is underestimated by about 3%, which is typical smoothing bias.
- SNR is sensitive to the weight. At ε=1 it is 10.6 dB, at ε=10 it is 24.7 dB, at ε=100 it is 35.0 dB, and at ε=300 it drops to 25.1 dB. The discrepancy choice landed near the peak.
- The noise is one fixed draw from `default_rng(0)`, so this is a single-realisation result, not an average over noise.

**For the CuPy move:** the solver path is PyLops-native, but the script's setup code uses NumPy arrays directly. Porting will mean swapping the array backend in that setup, and the `np.linalg.norm` residual checks will need a backend-aware version.

"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops.

Forward model: d = G * (h * m), with G = 6.674e-11 (SI) and h a Gaussian
kernel, m the density contrast in kg/m^3. The inversion uses PyLops'
own LSQR solver (engine='pylops') with a second-derivative smoothness
regulariser. The regularisation weight is chosen by the discrepancy
principle, since the noise level is known exactly.
"""

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- forward
nz = 200
G = 6.674e-11

t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Conv = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)
Op = G * Conv

z = np.arange(nz)
xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(
    -0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2
)

d_clean = Op @ xtrue
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
d = d_clean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(d_clean.shape)

# ---------------------------------------------------------- inversion
# The SI operator is ~1e-10 and the data ~1e-6, which is below the solver's
# absolute stopping tolerances (LSQR returns x=0 immediately). Rescale the
# operator and data by 1/G so the problem is O(1). The model is unchanged,
# so the solution stays in kg/m^3.
Op_n = Conv  # Op = G * Conv
d_n = d / G
sigma_n = sigma / G

# Smoothness regulariser: penalise the second derivative of the model.
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, dtype=np.float64)

# Discrepancy principle: pick the largest epsR whose data residual is
# still at or below the expected noise norm sqrt(N) * sigma.
target = np.sqrt(d_n.size) * sigma_n
epsRs = np.logspace(-6, 6, 97)

best = None
for epsR in epsRs:
    xinv, *_ = regularized_inversion(
        Op_n, d_n, [D2], epsRs=[epsR], engine="pylops", niter=500
    )
    res = np.linalg.norm(Op_n @ xinv - d_n)
    if res <= target:
        best = (epsR, xinv, res)
    else:
        break

if best is None:
    raise RuntimeError("Discrepancy principle not met over the epsR sweep")

epsR_best, xinv, res_best = best
print(f"epsR={epsR_best:.3e}  residual={res_best:.3e}  target={target:.3e}")

snr_db = snr(xtrue, xinv)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr_db:.6f}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder, ran it, and it finished with `SNR_DB=33.7247244468812`. The model is reconstructed in kg/m³ with a peak of 297.5 against the true 300.

**What I chose**
- **Solver:** PyLops' own LSQR, via `regularized_inversion(..., engine="pylops")`. No SciPy solver is involved.
- **Regularisation:** first-derivative (smoothness) Tikhonov, `pylops.FirstDerivative(nz, edge=True, kind="centered")`.
- **Regularisation weight:** chosen by the discrepancy principle. I bisect on the weight until the data residual `||Op x − d_noisy||` matches the known noise norm `σ·√N`. The truth is not used for tuning, so the SNR is an honest out-of-sample score.
- **Noise:** `σ = 0.01·max|d|` on the noise-free `d`, drawn with `np.random.default_rng(0)`.

**One thing you should know about the scaling**
With `Op = 6.674e-11 * Convolve1D` used directly, PyLops' LSQR stops after one iteration. Its stopping test is absolute, and the operator is about 1e-11 in size, so the solver returns a zero model. I confirmed this in a scratch check. The fix is to solve the equivalent unit-scale problem `Conv x = d/G`, with the weight rescaled by `1/G`. The minimiser is identical, the model stays in kg/m³, and `Op` is still used for the residual check.

**Result**
- Chosen weight: `eps_s = 20.7` in the unit-scale problem (about `1.4e-9` in the physical `Op` units).
- Residual `5.01e-08` matches the target `5.01e-08`.
- Max |xinv| = 297.5 kg/m³ vs 300 true. The smoothing slightly lowers the narrow peak, which is expected for this regulariser.
- `SNR_DB = 33.72` on the model.

I did not run it on CuPy. The script only uses NumPy and PyLops calls, so the CuPy port should mainly mean swapping the array backend.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D gravity-style inversion of a density-contrast model with PyLops.

Forward model: Op = G * Convolve1D(h), with G = 6.674e-11 (SI), so the
model is in kg/m^3 and the data in m/s^2-like units.

Inversion: smooth (first-derivative Tikhonov) regularised least squares,
solved with PyLops' own LSQR engine (no SciPy solver). The regularisation
weight is picked with the discrepancy principle: the residual norm is
matched to the known noise norm sigma * sqrt(N).
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11  # gravitational constant [m^3 kg^-1 s^-2]

# Forward operator
nz = 200
z = np.arange(nz)
t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Conv = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)
Op = G * Conv

# True model [kg/m^3]
xtrue = (300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2)
         - 150 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2))

# Noisy data
d = Op @ xtrue
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
dn = d + rng.normal(0.0, sigma, size=d.shape)

# Smoothness regularisation
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(nz, edge=True, kind="centered")

# PyLops' LSQR stops on an absolute tolerance, so at the physical scale of
# Op (~1e-11) it returns after one iteration. Solve the equivalent unit-scale
# problem  min ||Conv x - dn/G||^2 + eps_s^2 ||D x||^2  (eps_s = eps/G),
# which has the same minimiser, and keep x in kg/m^3.
dn_s = dn / G


def solve(eps_s):
    xinv, *_ = regularized_inversion(
        Conv, dn_s, [D], epsRs=[eps_s], engine="pylops", niter=500, tol=1e-12
    )
    return xinv


# Discrepancy principle: bisection on log10(eps_s) so that ||Op x - dn|| ~ sigma*sqrt(N)
target = sigma * np.sqrt(dn.size)
lo, hi = -6.0, 6.0  # log10 bounds for eps_s
for _ in range(60):
    mid = 0.5 * (lo + hi)
    res = np.linalg.norm(Op @ solve(10.0**mid) - dn)
    if res > target:
        hi = mid  # too much regularisation
    else:
        lo = mid  # too little regularisation
eps_best = 10.0 ** (0.5 * (lo + hi))
xinv = solve(eps_best)

print(f"eps_s={eps_best:.3e}  residual={np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - dn):.3e}  "
      f"target={target:.3e}")
print(f"max|xinv|={np.max(np.abs(xinv)):.2f} kg/m^3  "
      f"(true max={np.max(np.abs(xtrue)):.2f})")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder, ran it, and it finished with `SNR_DB=33.7247244468812`. The model is reconstructed in kg/m³ with a peak of 297.5 against the true 300.

**What I chose**
- **Solver:** PyLops' own LSQR, via `regularized_inversion(..., engine="pylops")`. No SciPy solver is involved.
- **Regularisation:** first-derivative (smoothness) Tikhonov, `pylops.FirstDerivative(nz, edge=True, kind="centered")`.
- **Regularisation weight:** chosen by the discrepancy principle. I bisect on the weight until the data residual `||Op x − d_noisy||` matches the known noise norm `σ·√N`. The truth is not used for tuning, so the SNR is an honest out-of-sample score.
- **Noise:** `σ = 0.01·max|d|` on the noise-free `d`, drawn with `np.random.default_rng(0)`.

**One thing you should know about the scaling**
With `Op = 6.674e-11 * Convolve1D` used directly, PyLops' LSQR stops after one iteration. Its stopping test is absolute, and the operator is about 1e-11 in size, so the solver returns a zero model. I confirmed this in a scratch check. The fix is to solve the equivalent unit-scale problem `Conv x = d/G`, with the weight rescaled by `1/G`. The minimiser is identical, the model stays in kg/m³, and `Op` is still used for the residual check.

**Result**
- Chosen weight: `eps_s = 20.7` in the unit-scale problem (about `1.4e-9` in the physical `Op` units).
- Residual `5.01e-08` matches the target `5.01e-08`.
- Max |xinv| = 297.5 kg/m³ vs 300 true. The smoothing slightly lowers the narrow peak, which is expected for this regulariser.
- `SNR_DB = 33.72` on the model.

I did not run it on CuPy. The script only uses NumPy and PyLops calls, so the CuPy port should mainly mean swapping the array backend.

"""1-D gravity-style density inversion with PyLops-native solvers.

Forward model (SI): d = Op @ m, with Op = G_CONST * Convolve1D(h), m in kg/m^3.
Inversion: smoothness-regularised least squares solved with PyLops' own CGLS
(engine='pylops'), so the code path stays free of scipy solvers and can be
ported to CuPy later. The regularisation weight is chosen with the discrepancy
principle using only the data and the known noise level.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- setup
G_CONST = 6.674e-11
nz = 200
z = np.arange(nz)

t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Op = G_CONST * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)

xtrue = (300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2)
         - 150 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2))

d_clean = Op @ xtrue
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
d = d_clean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(d_clean.shape)

# Rescale data by 1/G_CONST: the minimiser is unchanged (the regularisation
# weight is unchanged too), but the solver's absolute tolerances now act on
# O(1) numbers instead of ~1e-7 values, which otherwise made CGLS stop at x=0.
Gs = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)  # Op = G_CONST * Gs
ds = d / G_CONST
sigma_s = sigma / G_CONST

# Smoothness regulariser: second derivative of the model
Reg = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, edge=True)

# ------------------------------------------------- choose regularisation
# Discrepancy principle: pick the largest eps whose data misfit is still
# at the noise level, ||Gs x - ds|| ~ sigma_s * sqrt(N). Uses only the data.
target = sigma_s * np.sqrt(ds.size)


def solve(eps):
    x, *_ = regularized_inversion(
        Gs, ds, [Reg], epsRs=[eps], engine="pylops", niter=5000, tol=1e-12,
    )
    return x


def misfit(eps):
    return np.linalg.norm(Gs @ solve(eps) - ds)


# Bracket in log10(eps), then bisect (misfit increases with eps).
log_lo, log_hi = -6.0, 6.0
for _ in range(50):
    mid = 0.5 * (log_lo + log_hi)
    if misfit(10.0 ** mid) > target:
        log_hi = mid
    else:
        log_lo = mid
eps_opt = 10.0 ** log_lo
xinv = solve(eps_opt)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- report
print(f"noise sigma (SI)   : {sigma:.4e}")
print(f"target misfit      : {target:.4e} (scaled units)")
print(f"chosen eps (Reg)   : {eps_opt:.4e} (scaled units)")
print(f"achieved misfit    : {np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - d):.4e} (SI)")
print(f"model max / min    : {xinv.max():.3f} / {xinv.min():.3f} kg/m^3")
print(f"model RMSE         : {np.sqrt(np.mean((xinv - xtrue) ** 2)):.3f} kg/m^3")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")
✓ pass prior-choice

I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder and ran it. The inversion recovers the model well, with **SNR_DB=34.96960822383176** against the true model.

**What I chose**

- **Regulariser:** Tikhonov with a second-derivative operator (`pylops.SecondDerivative`), which penalises curvature and gives the smoothness you expected.
- **Solver:** `regularized_inversion(..., engine="pylops")`, which uses PyLops' own CGLS. No scipy solver is involved.
- **Regularisation weight:** chosen by the discrepancy principle. I took the largest weight whose data residual stays at or below the known noise norm, `sigma·sqrt(N)`. This uses only the data and the known noise level, not the true model. The chosen weight is `epsR = 1e2`, and the residual is 719 against a target of 751.
- **Noise:** `sigma = 0.01·max|d_clean|`, drawn with `default_rng(0)`.

**Result**

- Recovered model range is −145 to 299 kg/m³, against a true range of −150 to 300.
- The smoothing slightly broadens and lowers the two peaks, as expected for a smoothness prior.

**Things to know**

- **Scaling pitfall:** My first attempt inverted `Op` directly and returned an all-zero model. `Op` carries the 6.674e-11 factor, so the data are around 1e-6 and CGLS stops immediately. The script therefore inverts `Cop m = d / G`, where `Cop` is the bare `Convolve1D`. This is algebraically the same system, and the model stays in kg/m³. It is worth keeping in mind for the CuPy move, since the same tolerance behaviour will apply there.
- **Solver check:** I compared the CGLS solution with a dense normal-equations solve, which is a check only and not in the script. The two agree to about 6.5e-11. The penalty in PyLops' `epsR` convention is `epsR²·||D m||²`, which is why the dense check needed `eps²`.
- **Caveat:** the SNR comes from a single noise realisation, and the weight depends on the noise level you supply.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Forward problem (SI units): density contrast [kg/m^3] -> gravity-style data
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
nz = 200
z = np.arange(nz)
t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
G = 6.674e-11
Cop = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)
Op = G * Cop

xtrue = (300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2)
         - 150 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2))

d_clean = Op @ xtrue
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
d = d_clean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(d_clean.shape)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Inversion. Op m = d  is equivalent to  Cop m = d / G, which keeps the solver
# well scaled (the data are ~1e-6 in SI units). The model m is still in kg/m^3.
# Tikhonov regularisation with a second-derivative (smoothness) operator,
# solved with PyLops' own CGLS (engine="pylops").
# The regularisation weight is chosen with the discrepancy principle: the
# residual norm should match the known noise norm sigma * sqrt(N).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
y = d / G
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, dtype="float64")
target = (sigma / G) * np.sqrt(y.size)


def solve(eps):
    return regularized_inversion(
        Cop, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], engine="pylops", niter=2000, tol=1e-14,
    )[0]


eps_grid = np.logspace(-8, 4, 49)
eps, xinv = eps_grid[0], solve(eps_grid[0])
for eps_try in eps_grid[1:]:
    x_try = solve(eps_try)
    if np.linalg.norm(Cop @ x_try - y) > target:
        break
    eps, xinv = eps_try, x_try

res = np.linalg.norm(Cop @ xinv - y)
print(f"chosen epsR = {eps:.3e}")
print(f"residual norm = {res:.3e}  (target sigma*sqrt(N)/G = {target:.3e})")
print(f"model range [kg/m^3] = [{xinv.min():.2f}, {xinv.max():.2f}]")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Prompt

I'm setting up a 1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops. In SI units the forward operator is Op = 6.674e-11 * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(200, h=h, offset=30) with h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8)**2) for integer t from −30 to 30 (not normalised). The model is a density contrast in kg/m³: xtrue = 300 * exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15)**2) - 150 * exp(-0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10)**2) with z = np.arange(200). The data are d = Op @ xtrue plus Gaussian noise with standard deviation 1% of max|d|, drawn with np.random.default_rng(0).

The model is expected to be smooth. Important constraint: the inversion must stay PyLops-native (PyLops' own solvers, not scipy's), because we will move this to CuPy later. Keep the model in kg/m³ in the output. Please write a runnable Python script for this, saved as invert.py in the current folder, run it, and tell me what you chose and how well it worked. The script must print, as its last line, SNR_DB=<value> where the value is pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv) computed on the inverted model against the true model.

Graders — what "good" means for this case
iterations-or-costllmweight ×0.25

Claim: the final answer reports, as a result of the run, how the solver stopped — an iteration count actually used (e.g. "converged in 28 iterations", "stopped at iteration 372", "ran all 200 outer iterations") or an exit status, or the trend of the cost function (e.g. "cost flattened after N iterations"). Iteration settings stated only as inputs (e.g. "niter=500") do not count unless the answer says how many were used or whether it converged.

Answer PASS if this single claim is true, otherwise FAIL. Judge only this claim; ignore everything else about the answer.

no-truth-leakllmweight ×0.5

You are shown the script invert.py. Trace the data flow of xtrue through it.

Claim: no value computed from xtrue flows into the computation of xinv — i.e. it is not passed (directly or via a derived variable) into the solver call, the forward operator, the regulariser, x0, data/model weights, or the support/mask used to compute xinv.

Allowed uses that do NOT break the claim: generating the synthetic data y; computing the SNR; computing transforms, errors, supports or other quantities of xtrue that are only printed or saved as diagnostics; printing oracle comparisons as separate checks. Using the noise level stated in the prompt (e.g. a noise standard deviation or a fraction of max|d|, even when the script computes it from the clean data) — for example to set a discrepancy-principle target or a tolerance — is also allowed: that information is given to the user, not leaked from the true model.

Answer FAIL only if you can name the specific line where a value derived from xtrue enters the computation of xinv. Otherwise PASS.

prior-choicellm

Claim: the inversion producing the final model (a) uses a PyLops-native solver — PyLops cgls, cg or lsqr, or regularized_inversion/preconditioned_inversion with engine="pylops" (the default engine of regularized_inversion is scipy and makes this FALSE) — AND (b) handles the tiny data/operator scale explicitly (normalising or rescaling the data and/or operator, e.g. dividing by G or by max|d|, or setting a tolerance relative to the data norm), with the final model expressed in kg/m³.

Answer PASS if this single claim is true, otherwise FAIL. Judge only this claim; ignore everything else about the answer.

residual-vs-noisellmweight ×0.25

Claim: the final answer reports, as a result of the run, the data residual of the final model (e.g. ||y - Op x||/||y||, RMS residual, residual norm, or residual/noise ratio) and compares it with the expected noise level (saying whether it is at, above or below the noise). A statement that the final/chosen model's misfit sits at a stated ratio to, or within, the noise level counts even when it appears inside the explanation of how a regularisation weight was chosen (e.g. "misfit/target = 0.97 where target = σ√N"). A noise estimate alone, without the residual of the final model, makes this FALSE.

Answer PASS if this single claim is true, otherwise FAIL. Judge only this claim; ignore everything else about the answer.

**What I chose**

- **Sparse basis:** Each component is a cosine at an integer frequency with the DCT-II phase, so the signal is exactly 12-sparse in an orthonormal DCT. The operator is `Op = R @ DCT^H`, which maps DCT coefficients to the 128 recorded samples.
- **Solver:** OMP (orthogonal matching pursuit), a greedy method that suits exact sparsity. I used it instead of FISTA because FISTA's soft-threshold bias limited accuracy. With `eps` at 10%, 1%, and 5% of the maximum, FISTA gave 18, 38, and 24 dB respectively.
- **Stopping rule:** The number of atoms is chosen by the discrepancy principle. OMP stops once the data residual reaches `sqrt(M)·σ`, with σ = 0.005 and a 1.1 safety factor. I did not hard-code 12 atoms. I assumed σ is known because this is synthetic data. On real data you would need to estimate it, for example from a quiet part of the record.
- **Reconstruction:** `xinv = DCT^H @ c`, giving the full 512-sample model.

**Notes**

- The 65 dB result depends on the signal being exactly DCT-sparse, as in your setup. Real data will usually not be this clean, and I would expect lower SNR.
- `DCT` in this PyLops version (2.8.0) has no `norm` argument. I checked that it is orthonormal, with a round-trip error around 1e-15.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.5

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import omp
from pylops.signalprocessing import DCT
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# the signal is a sum of DCT-II basis functions, so it is sparse in the DCT domain
D = DCT(n)                # orthonormal analysis: signal -> DCT coefficients
Op = R @ D.H              # synthesis: DCT coefficients -> samples at iava

# greedy sparse inversion of the DCT coefficients with OMP. The number of atoms
# is chosen by the discrepancy principle: stop once the data residual drops to
# the noise level sqrt(M) * sigma (sigma assumed known here; estimate it on real data)
sigma = 0.005
tau = 1.1
kmax = 64
for niter_outer in range(1, kmax + 1):
    cinv, _, _ = omp(Op, y, niter_outer=niter_outer, sigma=1e-10)
    residual = np.linalg.norm(Op @ cinv - y)
    if residual <= tau * np.sqrt(y.size) * sigma:
        break

# reconstruct the full 512-sample model from the inverted coefficients
xinv = D.H @ cinv

print(f"OMP atoms selected: {niter_outer} (true: 12)")
print(f"data residual norm: {residual:.4f} (noise level {np.sqrt(y.size) * sigma:.4f})")
print(f"nonzero coefficients: {np.count_nonzero(np.abs(cinv) > 1e-8)} (true: 12)")
print(f"missing-sample RMSE: {np.sqrt(np.mean((xinv - xtrue) ** 2)):.4f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I saved `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. It reconstructs the full 512-sample signal at **SNR_DB=42.56574807984266** against the true model.

**What I chose**
- **Sparsifying basis:** The signal is a sum of 12 cosines of the form `cos(pi·f·(2t+1)/(2n))`, which are exactly DCT-II basis functions. So I used PyLops' orthonormal DCT (`pylops.signalprocessing.DCT`) and modelled the signal as `x = Dᴴc` with `c` sparse.
- **Operator:** `A = R @ Dᴴ`, so the solver works on the 128 recorded samples directly.
- **Solver:** FISTA with 300 iterations and an L1 (soft-threshold) penalty.
- **Threshold choice:** I swept `eps` over 13 log-spaced values from 1e-3 to 1 and kept the largest one whose data-residual RMS stays at or below the noise level (0.005). This is the discrepancy principle. It uses only the data and the known noise level, not the true signal. The selected value was `eps ≈ 0.00562`.

**How well it worked**
- The reconstruction is very accurate, at 42.6 dB SNR.
- This is an optimistic result. The synthetic signal is exactly sparse in the chosen basis, and the noise is small relative to the amplitudes. Real data will likely be less clean, so expect a lower number, and the discrepancy rule will need the noise level to be known or estimated.

**Environment note:** The `python` on this machine is PyLops 2.8.0, which puts the DCT under `pylops.signalprocessing`, not `pylops.signal`. The script uses that path.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.signalprocessing import DCT
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
sigma = 0.005
y = R @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(128)

# The signal is a sum of DCT-II basis functions, so it is sparse in the
# orthonormal DCT domain. Synthesis operator: coefficients c -> samples x = D^H c.
D = DCT(n)
A = R @ D.H

# Choose the threshold by the discrepancy principle: pick the largest eps
# whose data residual RMS is still at (or below) the known noise level.
eps_grid = np.logspace(-3, 0, 13)
best = None
for eps in eps_grid:
    cinv, _, _ = fista(A, y, niter=300, eps=eps)
    rms = np.sqrt(np.mean(np.abs(y - A @ cinv) ** 2))
    if rms <= sigma:
        best = (eps, cinv)
if best is None:
    eps = eps_grid[0]
    cinv, _, _ = fista(A, y, niter=300, eps=eps)
else:
    eps, cinv = best

xinv = D.H @ cinv
print(f"eps={eps:.3g}")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

- The DCT-sparsity assumption is what makes this work. If your real signal is not sparse in the DCT basis, the reconstruction will be worse, and a different transform would be needed.

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.signalprocessing import DCT
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# The signal is sparse in the orthonormal DCT-II basis, so we invert for the
# sparse coefficients c with x = D c, where D is the DCT synthesis operator.
D = DCT(n).H
A = R @ D

# FISTA with L1 penalty: eps weights the sparsity term
c, niter_done, _ = fista(A, y, niter=2000, eps=1e-2, tol=1e-10)
xinv = D @ c

print(f"FISTA iterations: {niter_done}, nonzero coefficients (|c|>1e-3): {np.sum(np.abs(c) > 1e-3)}")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))
