iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# the signal is sparse in the (orthonormal) DCT-II basis: x = D^H s
Dop = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(n)
Aop = R @ Dop.H

# sparse inversion of the DCT coefficients with FISTA
s, _, _ = pylops.optimization.sparsity.FISTA(Aop).solve(
    y, niter=2000, eps=1e-2, tol=1e-12
)
xinv = Dop.H @ s

print(f"nonzero coefficients: {np.sum(np.abs(s) > 1e-2)}")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
sigma = 0.005
y = R @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(128)

# The true signal is a sum of DCT-II basis functions, so it is sparse in the
# orthonormal DCT domain. Solve for sparse DCT coefficients c with x = D^H c:
#   min_c 0.5 * ||R D^H c - y||^2 + eps * ||c||_1   (FISTA)
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(n, type=2)
Op = R @ D.H

# Threshold from the noise level: eps ~ sigma * sqrt(2 log N)
eps = sigma * np.sqrt(2 * np.log(n))
c, _, _ = fista(Op, y, niter=2000, eps=eps, tol=1e-12)
xinv = D.H @ c

print(f"nonzero coeffs: {np.count_nonzero(np.abs(c) > 1e-3)} (true: 12)")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

- The DCT in this pylops version (2.8.0) has no `norm` argument. Its default is orthonormal, which I checked: the round-trip error is ~3e-16 and the norm is preserved.
- The recovery depends on the true signal being sparse in the DCT basis. Your real signal may not be, so check this on your own data before trusting the result.

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import fista

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
n = 512
t = np.arange(n)
freqs = rng.choice(np.arange(5, 200), 12, replace=False)
amps = rng.uniform(0.5, 2, 12) * rng.choice([-1, 1], 12)
xtrue = sum(a * np.cos(np.pi * f * (2 * t + 1) / (2 * n)) for a, f in zip(amps, freqs))

# only 128 randomly located samples were recorded
iava = np.sort(rng.choice(n, 128, replace=False))
R = pylops.Restriction(n, iava)
y = R @ xtrue + 0.005 * rng.standard_normal(128)

# The signal is sparse in the orthonormal DCT-II basis: the DCT coefficients
# are the model, and the synthesis operator D.H maps them back to samples.
D = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(n)
Op = R @ D.H

# Sparse inversion with FISTA (L1 regularization on the DCT coefficients)
eps = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(Op.H @ y))
cinv, _, _ = fista(Op, y, niter=500, eps=eps, tol=1e-12)

# Reconstructed full-length signal
xinv = D.H @ cinv

print(f"n_nonzero_coeffs={np.sum(np.abs(cinv) > 1e-3 * np.max(np.abs(cinv)))}")
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in `adjoint`. It uses `np.convolve` with `h`, but the adjoint of a causal convolution is a correlation. The forward operator is `y[n] = Σ_k h[k] x[n-k]`, so its matrix is lower-triangular Toeplitz with `A[i, j] = h[i-j]`. The transpose is `(Aᵀy)[j] = Σ_m h[m] y[j+m]`, which looks ahead in `y`. Convolution looks back, so it computes a different operator.

CGLS assumes the adjoint is the true transpose. With the wrong one it minimizes the wrong normal equations, so it stalls and returns a model unlike the truth. That is also why `dottest` fails.

The fix is to replace `adjoint` with a correlation:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    # (A^T y)[j] = sum_m h[m] * y[j+m]  -> correlation, not convolution
    return np.correlate(y, h, mode="full")[len(h) - 1 : len(h) - 1 + nt]
```

I checked this in the sandbox (PyLops 2.8.0):

- `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt)` returns `True`. With the original adjoint, the dot-test relative mismatch was about 145%.
- The corrected adjoint matches the explicit matrix `A.T` exactly.
- Your `cgls` call with `niter=100, damp=1e-3` gives a relative model error of about 0.016.

The forward function was already correct, so only the adjoint needs changing.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
## Diagnosis: the adjoint is wrong

The adjoint is the problem. `adjoint(y)` convolves `y` with `h`, which is the transpose only when `h` is symmetric. For a causal filter, the true adjoint of the truncated convolution is a correlation: `x[m] = Σₖ h[k]·y[m+k]`. Convolving the time-reversed `y` and reversing the result gives exactly that.

### Findings, ranked by impact

**1. Wrong adjoint (dot-test fails). Run.**
- Command: `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, 300, 300, raiseerror=False, verb=True)`
- Output: `Dot test failed, v^H(Opu)=-32.537… - u^H(Op^Hv)=-5.018…`. A second run with fresh random vectors also failed (`63.27` vs `-60.02`).
- Dense check: `np.linalg.norm(M.T - Op.H.todense()) = 38.71`, so the operator's adjoint is not the transpose of its matrix.
- Likely cause: the adjoint uses `np.convolve(y, h)` where it needs the correlation.
- Fix:
  ```python
  def adjoint(y):
      return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
  ```
- Tested: the dot-test passes (`69.18328104445507 vs 69.18328104445479`), and cgls with the same call now converges:
  - istop 1 after 34 iterations (broken: istop 2 after 100)
  - relative model error 0.0158 (broken: 3.19)
  - relative data misfit 0.0017 (broken: 6.79)
  - cost falls from 3.0 to 0.0051 (broken: it rises from 3.0 to 20.4)

**Mismatch with your description.** You said the cost "barely decreases". In my run with your code it actually increases from 2.9986 to 20.37 over 100 iterations, because the cgls updates are built on a wrong adjoint. If your cost history looks different, your code may differ from what you pasted.

**2. Shapes and dtypes. Run.**
- `Op.shape = (300, 300)`, `y` and `xtrue` are `(300,)` float64, and `y` is finite.
- No ordering, broadcasting, or dtype issue.

**3. Scaling and conditioning. Run (dense matrix).**
- Largest and smallest singular values: 6.46 and 0.538. Condition number 12.0. Well conditioned.
- `‖y‖ = 3.0`, `‖Opᴴy‖ = 13.8` (the broken adjoint). Nothing in the scaling needs fixing.
- `Op.eigs(neigs=1)` failed in this environment with an `AttributeError` inside SciPy's ARPACK wrapper (`'str' object has no attribute 'char'`). I used the dense matrix instead. This is an environment issue, not a PyLops finding.

**4. Stopping and regularisation. Run (after the fix).**
- Fixed run stops by `istop=1` at iteration 34, with final residual norm about 0.0051. The noise norm is about `1e-3·√300 = 0.0173`, so the residual is below the noise level. That is an estimate from the numbers above, not a measured result. With `damp=1e-3` there is mild overfitting risk, but the error is 1.6%, so I did not change the regularisation.

**Not run:**
- Per-block dot-test: there is only one operator and I tested it as a whole.
- Complex dtypes: none in this problem.
- NaN/Inf in internals: only `y` was checked for finiteness.
- CuPy/JAX backends: not used.
- Repeated dot-test runs: I did two runs of the broken operator, both failed. The fixed operator passed once with random vectors. I did not run the fixed dot-test repeatedly.

### Proposed change

Replace the adjoint in your script with the correlation version above. Nothing else needs to change. Keep the dot-test in your test suite: it catches this kind of error before the solver runs.

Environment note: `pylops.utils.dottest` in PyLops 2.8.0 has no `tol` argument. Use `rtol`/`atol`, and `raiseerror=False` if you want it to print instead of raise.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is the adjoint. `adjoint` is a second causal convolution, but the transpose of a causal convolution is an anti-causal correlation. Your operator is not self-adjoint, so cgls is solving the wrong normal equations, which explains the stall and the wrong model.

Forward: `z[n] = sum_k h[n-k] x[k]` for `k <= n`. Its transpose is `x_adj[k] = sum_{n>=k} h[n-k] y[n]`, which runs backward in time. The fix is to reverse the input, convolve, and reverse the output:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

I ran this in a scratch script to check:

- With your original adjoint, the dot test fails. `<Ax, y>` and `<x, A^T y>` disagree on random vectors (74.9 vs -33.3), and `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt)` reports failure.
- With the corrected adjoint, the dot test passes.
- Using the same data as your snippet (`rng = default_rng(3)`, same `xtrue`, noise `1e-3`), `cgls(..., niter=100, damp=1e-3)` gives a relative error of about 0.016.

If you want to avoid hand-writing the transpose, `scipy.linalg.toeplitz` builds the matrix and `.T` gives the adjoint directly. For a 300-sample problem that's cheap. Hand-written adjoints like this one are where dot tests earn their keep, so keep running `dottest` after any change to the forward model.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in `adjoint`. It uses the same causal convolution as `forward`, but the adjoint of a causal convolution is an anti-causal correlation. Because the adjoint isn't the transpose of the forward operator, the dot-product test fails and CGLS follows a wrong gradient, which is why the cost stalls.

For `y[n] = sum_k h[k] x[n-k]`, the transpose is `x[n] = sum_k h[k] y[n+k]`. You get that by flipping the input, convolving, and flipping the output back:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

I ran this in the sandbox with your setup:

- `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nt, nt)` passes with the corrected adjoint and fails with yours.
- CGLS with `niter=100, damp=1e-3` gives a relative error of about 0.016 against `xtrue`.

Your forward operator and the `[:nt]` truncation are correct, so only the adjoint needs changing. Run `dottest` after any change to a `FunctionOperator`, since a mismatched adjoint produces plausible-looking but wrong inversions like this one.

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in `adjoint`. It applies the same causal convolution as `forward`, but the adjoint of a causal filter is anti-causal: it correlates with `h` instead of convolving. Because `Op` is lower-triangular and not symmetric, `Op.H` is not `Op`, so the adjoint test fails and cgls is solving the normal equations with an operator that isn't the transpose of the forward model. That's why the cost stalls.

The fix is to flip the input and output around the convolution:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    # transpose of causal convolution: x_adj[j] = sum_k h[k] * y[j+k]
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

I checked this by running it (numpy and pylops are available in the sandbox):

- `dottest` with the original adjoint: **False**. With the fixed adjoint: **True**.
- cgls with the fixed adjoint, `niter=100`, `damp=1e-3`: relative model error **≈ 0.016**.

Your forward operator and the cgls call were fine. Only the adjoint needed changing. If you want the operator to stay correct for other filters, a more general approach is to build the matrix once with `scipy.linalg.toeplitz` and use it for both directions, or to check the adjoint with `dottest` whenever you write a new `FunctionOperator`.

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in the adjoint. `adjoint` calls `np.convolve` with the same filter, but the transpose of a causal convolution is a correlation: `(Aᵀy)[m] = Σⱼ h[j]·y[m+j]`. The convolution you wrote is not the transpose, so CGLS is minimizing with the wrong normal equations and stalls.

Fixed adjoint:

```python
def adjoint(y):
    # (A^T y)[m] = sum_j h[j] * y[m+j]: convolve the reversed y, then reverse back
    return np.convolve(y[::-1], h, mode="full")[:nt][::-1]
```

I checked this by running your code:

- Against the explicit matrix `A` built from `forward`, the corrected adjoint matches `Aᵀy` to about 1e-15. The original adjoint is off by about 4.7.
- `pylops.utils.dottest(Op, 300, 300)` returns `True` with the fix and `False` with the original.
- With the fix, the CGLS reconstruction has a relative error of about 0.016 against `xtrue`, using the same noise, `damp=1e-3`, and `niter=100`.

Your forward operator, the `FunctionOperator` call, and the `cgls` call were fine. Only the adjoint needed changing.
✓ pass no-solve-skill

Skill called 0x (expected 0..0)

Prompt

I wrapped my causal filter as a PyLops FunctionOperator to deconvolve with cgls, but cgls stalls: the cost barely decreases after the first few iterations and the model looks nothing like the truth. pylops.utils.dottest(Op, 300, 300) also fails. What's wrong with my code?

import numpy as np
import pylops

nt = 300
h = np.exp(-np.arange(30) / 6.0)  # causal, decaying filter

def forward(x):
    return np.convolve(x, h, mode="full")[:nt]

def adjoint(y):
    return np.convolve(y, h, mode="full")[:nt]

Op = pylops.FunctionOperator(forward, adjoint, nt, nt)

rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
xtrue = np.zeros(nt)
xtrue[[40, 90, 150, 220]] = [1.0, -0.7, 0.5, 0.9]
y = Op @ xtrue + 1e-3 * rng.standard_normal(nt)

xinv = pylops.cgls(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(nt), niter=100, damp=1e-3)[0]
print(np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: the forward operator is a causal convolution (np.convolve(x, h, "full")[:nt]), but the adjoint repeats the same convolution instead of correlating: the adjoint must use the time-reversed filter, e.g. np.convolve(y, h[::-1], "full")[len(h) - 1 : len(h) - 1 + nt] (or np.correlate(y, h, "full") with the matching slice), or the user can use pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D directly. With a correct adjoint cgls converges.

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It says the adjoint is wrong (not the transpose of the forward) and that this is why the dot-test fails and cgls stalls.
  • It identifies the missing time reversal / correlation (and the corresponding slice) and gives a corrected adjoint equivalent to the ground truth, or recommends Convolve1D.
  • It does not name the solver choice, damp, niter or noise as the main cause.

Otherwise FAIL.

PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: User has a working PyLops smooth-model inversion using regularized_inversion with [D2] and epsRs=[1.0] picked by eye; result looks too smooth. Wants an L-curve for epsRs and a recommended value. Script: Convolve1D with Gaussian h (offset=15), n=200, noise sigma 0.02, D2 = SecondDerivative(n), xinv = regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[1.0], iter_lim=200). {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /Users/mrava87/Desktop/OpenSource/pylops-agent-skills/plugins/pylops/skills/tune-regularization\n\nGoal: a **sweep script** in the user's project that evaluates a regularisation\nparameter over a range, applies one or more selection criteria, and reports the\nchosen value with a plot that justifies it. PyLops has no built-in L-curve / GCV /\ndiscrepancy helper, so the skill provides `reference/lcurve_template.py`.\n\nRequires a working inversion (operator passes the dot-test, solver runs). If there\nis none, use `solve-inverse-problem` first.\n\n## 0. Check the environment\n\nSame as the other PyLops skills: use the project's Python runner, check\n`pylops.__version__`, inspect solver signatures in the installed version.\n\n## 1. Identify the parameter(s)\n\nMap the user's solver to the knob being tuned and its meaning:\n\n| Solver | Parameter | Objective term |\n|---|---|---|\n| `cgls` / `lsqr` | `damp` | `damp² ‖x‖²` |\n| `regularized_inversion` | `epsRs=[ε₁, ε₂, …]` | `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` |\n| `normal_equations_inversion` | `epsI`, `epsRs` (and `epsNRs`) | `epsI² ‖x‖²`, `Σ εᵢ² ‖Rᵢ x‖²` (same scaling as `regularized_inversion`) |\n| `ista` / `fista` | `eps` | `eps ‖SOpᴴ x‖₁` (effective threshold is `eps·alpha/2`) |\n| `splitbregman` | `epsRL1s`, `epsRL2s`, `mu` | L1 and L2 weights, data weight |\n| `spgl1` | `sigma` (BPDN) or `tau` (LASSO) | residual bound or L1-ball radius |\n| any Krylov solver without explicit regularisation | `niter` | early stopping (semi-convergence) |\n\nWith more than one weight, tune one at a time (fix the others at a\nscale-based guess), or fix their ratio and sweep an overall factor. Do not\nlaunch a full grid over several parameters unless the problem is small.\n\n## 2. Pick the criterion\n\nChoose based on what the user knows:\n\n1. **Known noise level** (sigma per sample, or an estimate from a noise-only\n   window): **discrepancy principle** - choose the largest regularisation whose\n   data residual `‖y − Op x‖` is ≤ `τ · σ · sqrt(N)`, `τ ≈ 1.0–1.1`. For\n   `spgl1`, this is exactly `sigma = τ σ sqrt(N)`.\n2. **Synthetic with a true model**: maximise `pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, x)`\n   (report it, but warn it does not transfer 1:1 to field data).\n3. **Nothing known**: **L-curve** - plot `log ‖Rx‖` vs `log ‖y − Op x‖` and pick the\n   point of maximum curvature. Needs a wide enough range to show both branches.\n4. **Small problem** (`Op.todense()` affordable, N, M ≲ 3000) and quadratic\n   regularisation: **GCV** computed exactly on the dense matrices (implemented in\n   the template).\n\nReport more than one criterion when they are cheap; disagreement between them is\ninformation for the user.\n\n## 3. Write and run the sweep\n\nAdapt `reference/lcurve_template.py`:\n\n- replace `build_problem` with the user's operator, data and regularisation;\n- replace `solve(eps, x0)` with the user's solver call **with everything else\n  fixed** (same `niter`, tolerance, `x0` policy);\n- use a **log-spaced grid** spanning at least 4 orders of magnitude around a\n  scale-based guess (e.g. `ε₀ ≈ ‖Opᴴ y‖ / ‖Rᴴ R Opᴴ y‖` for quadratic terms, or\n  `eps₀ ≈ max|Opᴴ y|` scale for L1 thresholds, where larger values give all-zero\n  models);\n- solve from large to small ε and **warm start** from the previous solution\n  (`x0=...`) to save iterations - unless the solver ignores `x0`;\n- keep `niter` large enough that each solve has converged, otherwise the curve\n  measures iteration count, not regularisation (check a couple of cost curves);\n- for early stopping as regulariser, run once with a callback that stores\n  `x` every k iterations and compute the same criteria on iteration number.\n\nRun it, then look at the plot before trusting the automatic pick: an L-curve with\nno clear corner (common with L1 terms or very noisy data) should be reported as\nsuch, not forced. With derivative regularisers a second knee often appears at\nvery large ε (the model collapses into the null space of `R`, e.g. a linear\ntrend for `SecondDerivative`); it bends the other way and is not the L-curve\ncorner – the template's curvature orientation already excludes it.\n\n## 4. Hand off\n\nReport:\n\n- the parameter swept, the range and the number of solves;\n- the value picked by each criterion and the one recommended, with the reason;\n- the plot file(s) (L-curve, residual vs ε, SNR vs ε if available) and the\n  model at the chosen value next to one over- and one under-regularised model;\n- the exact line to change in the user's inversion script.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: Choose epsRs for regularized_inversion in the smooth-model convolution inversion script (Convolve1D + SecondDerivative, n=200, noise sigma 0.02). Prompt

My smooth-model inversion with PyLops works fine, but I picked epsRs=[1.0] by eye and the result looks a bit too smooth. How should I choose epsRs properly? Please make me an L-curve for it and tell me which value to use. Here's the script:

import numpy as np
import pylops

rng = np.random.default_rng(5)
n = 200
z = np.arange(n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * z / 80) + 0.5 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 120) / 6) ** 2)
t = np.arange(-15, 16)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 4) ** 2); h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=15)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.02 * rng.standard_normal(n)

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n)
xinv = pylops.regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[1.0], iter_lim=200)[0]
print(np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue))

  • The answer (with a script that was actually run in the trace) sweeps epsRs over a logarithmic range of values (at least ~8 values spanning several orders of magnitude) with the user's regularized_inversion setup.
  • For each value it computes the data residual norm and the regularisation norm (||D2 x||), and builds an L-curve (plot saved to a file and/or the values tabulated); it selects a value by a stated criterion (L-curve corner / maximum curvature, and/or the discrepancy principle with noise std 0.02).
  • It reports the recommended epsRs and the numbers backing it, and they do not visibly contradict the tool output in the trace (parts of the trace may be elided; do not fail for numbers you cannot find).
  • It keeps the user's solver and regulariser rather than re-designing the inversion (a brief side remark about alternatives is fine).

Otherwise FAIL.
